Linearly Implicit Finite Element Methods Approximating the Solution to the Nonlinear Schrödinger Equation with a Schamel-Type Nonlinearity
Authors: Panagiotis Paraschis, Georgios E. Zouraris
Published: 2026

ABSTRACT
We consider an initial- and Dirichlet boundary- value problem for a nonlinear Schrödinger equation of the form \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u_t=\,\textrm{i}\,{\varDelta }u+\textrm{i}\,V\,u+\textrm{i}\,\mu \,|u|^{\beta }\,u+f$$\end{document}ut=iΔu+iVu+iμ|u|βu+f over \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$[0,T]\times {\varOmega }$$\end{document}[0,T]×Ω, where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T>0$$\end{document}T>0, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\varOmega }\subset {\mathbb {R}}^d$$\end{document}Ω⊂Rd for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$d\in \{1,2,3\}$$\end{document}d∈{1,2,3}, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta \in (0,1)$$\end{document}β∈(0,1), V is a real-valued time-independent potential and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu $$\end{document}μ is a nonzero real number. The solution to the problem is approximated by the Linearized Backward Euler finite element (LBEFE) method which is dissipative and the Linearized Crank–Nicolson finite element (LCNFE) one which is conservative. Letting \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tau $$\end{document}τ be the time-step and h be the width of the finite element partition of the space domain, we provide an optimal order \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$O(\tau +h^2)$$\end{document}O(τ+h2) error estimate in the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^2$$\end{document}L2 norm for both methods, and an \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$O(\tau ^{\alpha }+h)$$\end{document}O(τα+h) error estimate in the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H^1$$\end{document}H1 norm, where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha =\frac{3}{4}$$\end{document}α=34 in the (LBEFE) method and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha =\frac{1}{2}$$\end{document}α=12 in the (LCNFE) one. For \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$d=1$$\end{document}d=1, no CFL conditions are imposed, while for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$d=2$$\end{document}d=2 or 3, a mesh condition of the form \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\big (h^{\frac{2-d}{2}}\,|\ln (h)|^{\frac{d-1}{d}} \,\tau ^{\alpha }+h^{2-\frac{d}{2}}\big )=O(1)$$\end{document}(h2-d2|ln(h)|d-1dτα+h2-d2)=O(1) is required. Finally, with results from numerical experiments, we investigate the performance of the methods proposed and analyzed.

Introduction

Formulation of the Problem

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T>0$$\end{document}T>0 be a final time, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$d\in \{1,2,3\}$$\end{document}d∈{1,2,3}, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\varOmega }\subset {\mathbb {R}}^d$$\end{document}Ω⊂Rd be a bounded convex domain with smooth or polygonal boundary \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\partial {\varOmega }$$\end{document}∂Ω, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta \in (0,1)$$\end{document}β∈(0,1), \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu $$\end{document}μ be a nonzero real number and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathfrak g}:{\mathbb {R}}^2\rightarrow {\mathbb {C}}$$\end{document}g:R2→C be a complex-valued function defined by1.1\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} {\mathfrak g}(x)=\textrm{i}\,\mu \,|x|_{\scriptscriptstyle \mathsf E}^{\beta }\,(x_1+\textrm{i}\,x_2) \quad \forall \,x\in {\mathbb {R}}^2, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}g(x)=iμ|x|Eβ(x1+ix2)∀x∈R2,where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$|\cdot |_{\scriptscriptstyle \mathsf E}$$\end{document}|·|E denotes the usual Euclidean norm on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathbb {R}}^2$$\end{document}R2. Then, we consider the model initial- and Dirichlet boundary- value problem of finding a function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u:[0,T]\times {\overline{{\varOmega }}}\rightarrow {\mathbb {C}}$$\end{document}u:[0,T]×Ω¯→C such that1.2\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} u_t&=\,\textrm{i}\,{\varDelta }u+\textrm{i}\,V\,u+g(u)+f\quad \forall \,(t,x)\in (0,T]\times {\varOmega }, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ut=iΔu+iVu+g(u)+f∀(t,x)∈(0,T]×Ω,1.3\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}&u(t,\cdot )|_{\scriptscriptstyle \partial D}=\,0\qquad \forall \,t\in (0, T], \end{aligned}$$\end{document}u(t,·)|∂D=0∀t∈(0,T],1.4\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} u(0,x)&=\,u_0(x)\qquad \forall \,x\in {\overline{{\varOmega }}}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}u(0,x)=u0(x)∀x∈Ω¯,where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u_0:{\overline{{\varOmega }}}\rightarrow {\mathbb {C}}$$\end{document}u0:Ω¯→C is a smooth complex-valued function with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u_0|_{\scriptscriptstyle \partial {\varOmega }}=0$$\end{document}u0|∂Ω=0, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f:[0,T]\times {\overline{{\varOmega }}}\rightarrow {\mathbb {C}}$$\end{document}f:[0,T]×Ω¯→C, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$V:{\overline{{\varOmega }}}\rightarrow {\mathbb {R}}$$\end{document}V:Ω¯→R a bounded, real-valued function, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$g:{\mathbb {C}}\rightarrow {\mathbb {C}}$$\end{document}g:C→C is a complex-valued function given by1.5\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} g(z):={\mathfrak g}(\textsf{Re}(z),\textsf{Im}(z))=\textrm{i}\,\mu \,|z|^{\beta }\,z \quad \forall \,z\in {\mathbb {C}}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}g(z):=g(Re(z),Im(z))=iμ|z|βz∀z∈C.For \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta =\frac{1}{2}$$\end{document}β=12, we obtain the Schamel-nonlinear Schrödinger equation which appears as a mathematical model for the propagation of waves in a multicomponent dense plasma (see, e.g., [18, 30, 31]), while the Schamel-nonlinearity has been proposed earlier, in the context of the Korteweg de Vries equation, for the investigation of one-dimensional ion-acoustic waves due to resonant electrons [34]. Moreover, a Schamel-type nonlinearity, with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta =\tfrac{2}{3}$$\end{document}β=23, appears in [7] in a Schrödinger-Poisson-X\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha $$\end{document}α model.

We refer the reader to [13, 28] and [40], and the references therein, for mathematical results on the problem above. To the knowledge of the authors, the existing bibliography on the numerical approximation of the problem above is limited and includes time-splitting methods (see [15, 23]), time-splitting pseudospectral methods (see, e.g. [5, 6, 8, 24]), the Backward Euler finite difference method [22], a linearized Crank–Nicolson finite difference method [30], and space-time finite element methods [2]. Here, we are interested in the approximation of the solution to the problem by linearly implicit time-stepping methods, because they avoid the use of solvers of nonlinear systems of algebraic equations, and by the usual conforming finite element method in space, because, in contrast to the finite difference method, it is applicable to general domains, and, in contrast to the splitting methods, is independent of the domain-dependent spectrum of the Laplace operator.

Notation and Preliminaries

We denote by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H^{\kappa }({\varOmega })$$\end{document}Hκ(Ω) the Sobolev space of complex-valued functions which belong, along with their generalized derivatives up to order \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\kappa $$\end{document}κ, to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^2({\varOmega })$$\end{document}L2(Ω), and by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Vert \cdot \Vert _{\kappa }$$\end{document}‖·‖κ its usual norm, i.e. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Vert v\Vert _{\kappa }:=\left( \, \sum _{\beta _{\star }\in {\mathbb {N}}^d_0,\,|\beta _{\star }|\le \kappa } \Vert \partial ^{\beta _{\star }}v\Vert ^2\,\right) ^{\scriptscriptstyle 1/2}$$\end{document}‖v‖κ:=∑β⋆∈N0d,|β⋆|≤κ‖∂β⋆v‖21/2 for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v\in H^{\kappa }({\varOmega })$$\end{document}v∈Hκ(Ω). For later use, we recall the following useful identity1.6\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \textsf{Re}\left[ (v_2-v_1,v_2)\right] =\tfrac{1}{2}\,\left( \,\Vert v_2\Vert ^2-\Vert v_1\Vert ^2 +\Vert v_2-v_1\Vert ^2\,\right) \quad \forall \,v_1,v_2\in L^2({\varOmega }), \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Re(v2-v1,v2)=12‖v2‖2-‖v1‖2+‖v2-v1‖2∀v1,v2∈L2(Ω),and we show below that g enjoys a very useful global Lipschitz-type inequality.

Lemma 1.1

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathfrak g}$$\end{document}g and g be given by (1.1) and (1.5), respectively. Then, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathfrak g}\in C^1({\mathbb {R}}^2)$$\end{document}g∈C1(R2) and it holds that1.7\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} |g(v)-g(w)|\le \,2\,|\mu |\,(1+\beta ) \,(|v|^{\beta }+|w|^{\beta })\,|v-w| \quad \forall \,v,w\in {\mathbb {C}}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}|g(v)-g(w)|≤2|μ|(1+β)(|v|β+|w|β)|v-w|∀v,w∈C.

Proof

It is obvious that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathfrak g}(x)=\textrm{i}\,\mu \,\left[ \Phi (x_1,x_2)+\textrm{i}\, \Phi (x_2,x_1)\right] $$\end{document}g(x)=iμΦ(x1,x2)+iΦ(x2,x1) for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x\in {\mathbb {R}}^2$$\end{document}x∈R2, where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Phi :{\mathbb {R}}^2\rightarrow {\mathbb {R}}$$\end{document}Φ:R2→R is a function defined by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Phi (y)=y_1\,|y|_{\scriptscriptstyle \mathsf E}^\beta $$\end{document}Φ(y)=y1|y|Eβ for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$y\in {\mathbb {R}}^2$$\end{document}y∈R2.

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x^{\star }\in {\mathbb {R}}^2$$\end{document}x⋆∈R2 with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x^{\star }\not =(0,0)$$\end{document}x⋆≠(0,0). It is obvious that the first order partial derivatives \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\partial _{x_1}\Phi (x^{\star })$$\end{document}∂x1Φ(x⋆) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\partial _{x_2}\Phi (x^{\star })$$\end{document}∂x2Φ(x⋆) exist and are given by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\partial _{x_1}\Phi (x^{\star })=|x^{\star }|_{\scriptscriptstyle \mathsf E}^{\beta } +\beta \,(x^{\star }_1)^2\,|x^{\star }|_{\scriptscriptstyle \mathsf E}^{\beta -2}$$\end{document}∂x1Φ(x⋆)=|x⋆|Eβ+β(x1⋆)2|x⋆|Eβ-2 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\partial _{x_2}\Phi (x^{\star })=\beta \,x^{\star }_1 \,x^{\star }_2\,|x^{\star }|_{\scriptscriptstyle \mathsf E}^{\beta -2}$$\end{document}∂x2Φ(x⋆)=βx1⋆x2⋆|x⋆|Eβ-2. Remark 1.1

Observing that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tfrac{|\partial _{x_1}\Phi (\varepsilon ,0) -\partial _{x_1}\Phi (0,0)|}{|\varepsilon |} =(1+\beta )\,|\varepsilon |^{\beta -1}$$\end{document}|∂x1Φ(ε,0)-∂x1Φ(0,0)||ε|=(1+β)|ε|β-1 is not bounded for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varepsilon $$\end{document}ε close to zero, we conclude that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\partial _{x_1}\Phi $$\end{document}∂x1Φ could not be a Lipschitz function around (0, 0) and that the second order partial derivative \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\partial _{x_1}^2\Phi $$\end{document}∂x12Φ is not defined at (0, 0). Thus, we can not provide function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathfrak g}$$\end{document}g with higher regularity properties over a domain that contains the origin.

The time-discretization of the problem will be based on a uniform partition of [0, T] with nodes \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(t_n)_{n=0}^{\scriptscriptstyle N}$$\end{document}(tn)n=0N defined by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t_n:=n\,\tau $$\end{document}tn:=nτ for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n=0,\dots , N$$\end{document}n=0,⋯,N, where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tau :=\frac{T}{N}$$\end{document}τ:=TN is the time-step. Also, given a set of functions \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\Upsilon ^n)_{n=0}^{\scriptscriptstyle N}$$\end{document}(Υn)n=0N corresponding to the nodes \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(t_n)_{n=0}^{\scriptscriptstyle N}$$\end{document}(tn)n=0N, we introduce the discrete time-derivatives \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\partial _{\tau }\Upsilon ^n:=\tfrac{\Upsilon ^{n}-\Upsilon ^{n-1}}{\tau }$$\end{document}∂τΥn:=Υn-Υn-1τ and the time-averages \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathcal {A}}_{\tau }\Upsilon ^n:= \tfrac{\Upsilon ^{n}+\Upsilon ^{n-1}}{2}$$\end{document}AτΥn:=Υn+Υn-12 for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n=1,\dots ,N$$\end{document}n=1,⋯,N. Later, in the error estimation, we will often arrive at a recursive inequality of the form1.16\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} q_{\scriptscriptstyle A}^{n}\le \,e^{{\mathfrak c}_{\star }\tau } \,q_{\scriptscriptstyle A}^{n-1}+q^n_{\scriptscriptstyle B},\quad n=1,\dots ,N, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}qAn≤ec⋆τqAn-1+qBn,n=1,⋯,N,where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathfrak c}_{\star }$$\end{document}c⋆, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(q_{\scriptscriptstyle A}^n)_{n=0}^{\scriptscriptstyle N}$$\end{document}(qAn)n=0N and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(q_{\scriptscriptstyle B}^n)_{n=1}^{\scriptscriptstyle N}$$\end{document}(qBn)n=1N are non-negative constants. Then, by induction, we can establish that1.17\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} q_{\scriptscriptstyle A}^n\le \,e^{{\mathfrak c}_{\star }\,t_n} \,\left( q_{\scriptscriptstyle A}^0+\sum _{\ell =1}^nq_{\scriptscriptstyle B}^{\ell }\right) , \quad n=1,\dots ,N. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}qAn≤ec⋆tnqA0+∑ℓ=1nqBℓ,n=1,⋯,N.

Linearly Implicit Finite Element Methods

We formulate below two linearly implicit finite element methods approximating the solution u to the problem (1.2)-(1.5) over the nodes \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(t_n)_{n=0}^{\scriptscriptstyle N}$$\end{document}(tn)n=0N.

The Linearized Backward Euler Finite Element Method

Motivated by [12], we formulate below the Linearized Backward Euler Finite Element (LBEFE) method consisting of the following steps:

Step LBE0. Set1.18\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \textsf{U}_h^0:=\textsf{P}_h(u_0). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Uh0:=Ph(u0).Step LBE1. Compute \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textsf{U}_h^1\in {\textsf{S}_h}$$\end{document}Uh1∈Sh such that1.19\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \partial _{\tau }\textsf{U}_h^{1}+\textrm{i}\,{\varDelta }_h(\textsf{U}_h^1) =\textsf{P}_h\left[ \textrm{i}\,\left( V+\mu \,|u_0|^{\beta }\right) \,\textsf{U}_h^1 +f(t_1,\cdot )\right] . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∂τUh1+iΔh(Uh1)=PhiV+μ|u0|βUh1+f(t1,·).Step LBE2. For \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n=2,\dots ,N$$\end{document}n=2,⋯,N, find \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textsf{U}_h^{n}\in {\textsf{S}_h}$$\end{document}Uhn∈Sh such that1.20\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \partial _{\tau }\textsf{U}_h^n +\textrm{i}\,{\varDelta }_h(\textsf{U}_h^n) =\textsf{P}_h\left[ \textrm{i}\,\left( V+\mu \,|\textsf{U}_h^{n-1}|^{\beta }\right) \,\textsf{U}_h^n +f(t_n,\cdot )\right] . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∂τUhn+iΔh(Uhn)=PhiV+μ|Uhn-1|βUhn+f(tn,·).

Remark 1.2

The linearization of g(u) at the core of the (LBEFE) method (see (1.20)) has the form \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textsf{g}_{\texttt {LBE}}(\textsf{U}_h^{n-1},\textsf{U}_h^n)$$\end{document}gLBE(Uhn-1,Uhn), where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textsf{g}_{\texttt {LBE}}(z_1,z_2):=\textrm{i}\,\mu \,|z_1|^{\beta }\,z_2$$\end{document}gLBE(z1,z2):=iμ|z1|βz2 for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$z_1,z_2\in {\mathbb {C}}$$\end{document}z1,z2∈C.

Remark 1.3

Taking into account that V is a real-valued function and using (1.6), we, easily, conclude that:1.21\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Vert \textsf{U}_h^n\Vert ^2+\Vert \textsf{U}_h^n-\textsf{U}_h^{n-1}\Vert ^2=\Vert \textsf{U}_h^{n-1}\Vert ^2 +2\,\tau \,\textsf{Re}\left[ \left( f(t_n,\cdot ),\textsf{U}_h^n\right) \right] ,\quad n=1,\dots ,N, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}‖Uhn‖2+‖Uhn-Uhn-1‖2=‖Uhn-1‖2+2τRef(tn,·),Uhn,n=1,⋯,N,which reads that the (LBEFE) method is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^2({\varOmega })$$\end{document}L2(Ω)-dissipative, i.e. if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f\equiv 0$$\end{document}f≡0, then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Vert \textsf{U}_h^n\Vert \le \Vert \textsf{U}_h^{n-1}\Vert $$\end{document}‖Uhn‖≤‖Uhn-1‖ for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n=1,\dots ,N$$\end{document}n=1,⋯,N. Moreover, since the (LBEFE) method is linearly implicit, relation (1.21) yields the existence and uniqueness of the the corresponding numerical approximations.

The Linearized Crank–Nicolson Finite Element Method

In the sequel, we formulate the Linearized Crank–Nicolson Finite Element (LCNFE) method which is a finite element version of the finite difference method proposed in [30] and has the following algorithm:

Step LCNFE0. Set1.22\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \textsf{W}_h^0:=\textsf{R}_h(u_0). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Wh0:=Rh(u0).Step LCNFE1. Compute \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textsf{W}_h^{1}\in {\textsf{S}_h}$$\end{document}Wh1∈Sh such that1.23\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \partial _{\tau }\textsf{W}_h^1 +\textrm{i}\,{\varDelta }_h\left( {\mathcal {A}}_{\tau }\textsf{W}_h^1\right) =\textsf{P}_h\left[ \textrm{i}\,\left( V+\mu \,|u_0|^{\beta }\right) \,{\mathcal {A}}_{\tau }\textsf{W}_h^1 +\tfrac{f(t_1,\cdot )+f(t_{0},\cdot )}{2}\right] . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∂τWh1+iΔhAτWh1=PhiV+μ|u0|βAτWh1+f(t1,·)+f(t0,·)2.Step LCNFE2. For \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n=2,\dots ,N$$\end{document}n=2,⋯,N, find \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textsf{W}_h^{n}\in {\textsf{S}_h}$$\end{document}Whn∈Sh such that1.24\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \partial _{\tau }\textsf{W}_h^n +\textrm{i}\,{\varDelta }_h\left( {\mathcal {A}}_{\tau }\textsf{W}_h^n\right) =\textsf{P}_h\left[ \textrm{i}\,\left( V+\mu \,|\textsf{W}_h^{n-1}|^{\beta }\right) \,{\mathcal {A}}_{\tau }\textsf{W}_h^n +\tfrac{f(t_n,\cdot )+f(t_{n-1},\cdot )}{2}\right] . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∂τWhn+iΔhAτWhn=PhiV+μ|Whn-1|βAτWhn+f(tn,·)+f(tn-1,·)2.

Remark 1.4

The linearization of g(u) at the core of the (LCNFE) method (see (1.24)) has the form \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textsf{g}_{\texttt {LCN}}(\textsf{W}_h^{n-1},\textsf{W}_h^n)$$\end{document}gLCN(Whn-1,Whn), where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textsf{g}_{\texttt {LCN}}(z_1,z_2):=\textrm{i}\,\mu \,|z_1|^{\beta }\,\frac{z_1+z_2}{2} =\frac{1}{2}\,\left[ g(z_1)+g_{\texttt {LBE}}(z_1,z_2)\right] $$\end{document}gLCN(z1,z2):=iμ|z1|βz1+z22=12g(z1)+gLBE(z1,z2) for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$z_1,z_2\in {\mathbb {C}}$$\end{document}z1,z2∈C.

Remark 1.5

Since V is a real-valued function, we, easily, arrive at1.25\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Vert \textsf{W}_h^n\Vert ^2=\Vert \textsf{W}_h^{n-1}\Vert ^2 +2\,\tau \,\textsf{Re}\left[ \left( \tfrac{f(t_n,\cdot )+f(t_{n-1},\cdot )}{2}, {\mathcal {A}}_{\tau }\textsf{W}_h^n\right) \right] ,\quad n=1,\dots ,N, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}‖Whn‖2=‖Whn-1‖2+2τRef(tn,·)+f(tn-1,·)2,AτWhn,n=1,⋯,N,which yields that the (LCNFE) method is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^2({\varOmega })$$\end{document}L2(Ω)-conservative, i.e. if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f\equiv 0$$\end{document}f≡0, then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Vert \textsf{W}_h^n\Vert =\Vert \textsf{R}_h(u_0)\Vert $$\end{document}‖Whn‖=‖Rh(u0)‖ for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n=0,\dots ,N$$\end{document}n=0,⋯,N. Also, since the (LCNFE) method is linearly implicit, relation (1.25) yields that the corresponding numerical approximations are well-defined.

Motivation and Main Results

The semilinear term of the nonlinear Schrödinger equation has, usually, the form \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$g(z)=\textrm{i}\,\phi (|z|^2)\,z$$\end{document}g(z)=iϕ(|z|2)z, where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\phi $$\end{document}ϕ is a polynomial (see, e.g. [9, 14, 32]) or a \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C^2([0,+\infty ))$$\end{document}C2([0,+∞)) function (see, e.g. [4, 11, 12, 25, 39, 41, 43]). This is not the case here, because \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\phi (s)=\mu \,s^{\frac{\beta }{2}}$$\end{document}ϕ(s)=μsβ2 for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$s\ge 0$$\end{document}s≥0, which is not differentiable at zero. The latter limited regularity of the non-linear part of the semilinear term makes the error estimation of a numerical method approximating the solution to the problem challenging, because the results obtained in the literature are based on the assumption that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\phi $$\end{document}ϕ is once or twice differentiable, or, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\phi $$\end{document}ϕ is locally Lipschitz on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$[0,+\infty )$$\end{document}[0,+∞), or g has \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C^2$$\end{document}C2 regularity (see, e.g., [1, 3, 4, 12, 16, 19, 21, 33, 35, 36, 39, 41–43]).

Here, our aim is to contribute to the numerical approximation of the nonlinear Schrödinger equation with a Schamel-type nonlinearity focusing on the development of a convergence analysis for the (LCNFE) and the (LBEFE) methods. Since both are linearly implicit, in order to avoid the enforcement of a CFL condition in their error analysis, it sounds natural to move along the lines of a technique proposed in [41] (see also [4, 26]), which consists of the formulation and the convergence analysis of the corresponding time discrete method. Unfortunately, this approach fails to reach the aforementioned target, because the linearizations \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textsf{g}_{\texttt {LBE}}$$\end{document}gLBE and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textsf{g}_{\texttt {LCN}}$$\end{document}gLCN of g, used in the methods under investigation (see Remark 1.2 and Remark 1.4), are not locally Lipschitz on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathbb {C}}^2$$\end{document}C2. In particular, it is not possible to show that the error between the fully discrete approximations and the elliptic projection of the time discrete approximations is a pure space error, i.e. that is independent of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tau $$\end{document}τ. This situation led us to follow the traditional way of estimating the error between the fully discrete approximations and the elliptic projection of the solution to the problem, having in our mind that mesh conditions may not be avoided.

To investigate the convergence of the proposed methods, we introduce a modified version of them by (3.10)-(3.12) and (3.34)-(3.36), where the linearizations \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textsf{g}_{\texttt {LBE}}$$\end{document}gLBE and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textsf{g}_{\texttt {LCN}}$$\end{document}gLCN are mollified properly by using a cut-off \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textsf{G}_{\lambda }$$\end{document}Gλ of the identity function (see (3.1)) that depends on a positive parameter \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda $$\end{document}λ (see, e.g. [43]). Choosing a large enough value \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\lambda _{\star }}$$\end{document}λ⋆ of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda $$\end{document}λ, we derive, without imposing any CFL condition, an optimal order \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$O(\tau +h^2)$$\end{document}O(τ+h2) error estimate in the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^2({\varOmega })$$\end{document}L2(Ω) norm for both modified methods, and an \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$O(\tau ^{\alpha }+h)$$\end{document}O(τα+h) error estimate in the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H^1({\varOmega })$$\end{document}H1(Ω) norm, where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha =\tfrac{3}{4}$$\end{document}α=34 for the modified (LBEFE) method and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha =\frac{1}{2}$$\end{document}α=12 for the modified (LCNFE) one. Assuming that h and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tau $$\end{document}τ are small enough, and using the available \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H^1({\varOmega })$$\end{document}H1(Ω) error estimates, we are able to conclude, for both methods, that the modified approximations are bounded in the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^{\infty }({\varOmega })$$\end{document}L∞(Ω) norm by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\lambda _{\star }}$$\end{document}λ⋆, without further conditions when \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$d=1$$\end{document}d=1, and under a rather mild mesh condition when \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$d=2$$\end{document}d=2 or 3, which follows by imposing an inverse inequality relating the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^{\infty }({\varOmega })$$\end{document}L∞(Ω) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H^1({\varOmega })$$\end{document}H1(Ω) norms (see (1.13)). Then, the mollifier acts as an identity (see (3.3)), resulting that the modified approximations are identical to those of the corresponding unmodified ones, and therefore their convergence properties are common. To the knowledge of the authors, it is the first time in the literature that error estimates for standard conforming finite element methods approximating the solution to (1.2) are provided.

We note that, for both methods, the order of the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H^1({\varOmega })$$\end{document}H1(Ω)-convergence with respect to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tau $$\end{document}τ is suboptimal because the linearization terms \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textsf{g}_{\texttt {LBE}}$$\end{document}gLBE and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textsf{g}_{\texttt {LCN}}$$\end{document}gLCN are not smooth enough, but we were not able to provide a numerical evidence of it (see Section 4) and thus we do not know if it is sharp or not. Moreover, we show that the order of convergence in the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H^1({\varOmega })$$\end{document}H1(Ω) norm of the (LBEFE) method is higher than that of the (LCNFE) method. The latter result follows because we are able to develop a stability argument for the (LBEFE) method which is based on (1.6) and limits the loss of the convergence rate due to the low regularity of the nonlinear term (cf. Theorem 1.1 in [27]).

The crucial step in our analysis is to overcome the loss of the local Lipschitz property by the linearizations \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textsf{g}_{\texttt {LBE}}$$\end{document}gLBE and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textsf{g}_{\texttt {LCN}}$$\end{document}gLCN. Since, each linearization follows from g(u) by approximating explicitly its nonlinear part and implicitly its linear one, a combination of the locally Lipschitz property (1.7) of g with a standard use of the Taylor formula allows us to arrive at an estimate of the consistency error of the corresponding time discretization (see Section 2). Finally, having the consistency error estimation as a compass, we succeed to provide convergence of the modified methods by handling efficiently the error between the linearized term and the corresponding linearized term in the consistency error equation which depends only on the exact solution to the problem.

Now, let us present some references related to the numerical approximation of the solution to the problem we consider here. The weak convergence of a Backward Euler finite difference scheme over \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathbb {R}}$$\end{document}R is discussed in [22], under the CFL condition \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tau =O(h^2)$$\end{document}τ=O(h2). For the time-splitting method over the whole space \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathbb {R}}^d$$\end{document}Rd formulated in [23], a convergence analysis has been developed by the authors of [15], that concludes an \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$O(\tau ^{\frac{1}{2}})$$\end{document}O(τ12) error estimate in the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^2$$\end{document}L2-norm, when the initial data belong to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H^1$$\end{document}H1. In [8], when Dirichlet boundary conditions are imposed, a first order, time-splitting method combined by a sine pseudospectral space discretization is proposed, and a low regularity \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$O(\tau ^{\frac{1+\beta }{2}}+h^{1+\beta })$$\end{document}O(τ1+β2+h1+β) error estimate in the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^2$$\end{document}L2-norm is provided, which is based on an \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varepsilon $$\end{document}ε-regularization of g around the origin. In our opinion, due to the local Lipschitz property (1.7), no regularization of the nonlinear term is required and its use may affect the results of the error analysis. For the method proposed and analyzed in [8], when periodic boundary conditions are imposed, an \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$O(\tau +h^2)$$\end{document}O(τ+h2) error estimate in the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^2$$\end{document}L2-norm and an \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$O(\tau ^{\frac{1}{2}}+h)$$\end{document}O(τ12+h) error estimate in the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H^1$$\end{document}H1-norm is shown in [6] under the CFL condition \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tau =O(h^2)$$\end{document}τ=O(h2), and an \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$O(\tau +h^m)$$\end{document}O(τ+hm) error estimate in the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^2$$\end{document}L2-norm and an \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$O(\tau ^{\frac{1}{2}}+h^{m-1})$$\end{document}O(τ12+hm-1) in the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H^1$$\end{document}H1-norm is provided in [24], where m indicates the space regularity of the solution. Also, space-time finite element methods are proposed and numerically tested in [2], when periodic boundary conditions are imposed without providing an error analysis. Our work has been motivated by [30], where a first order in time and second order in space, linearized Crank–Nicolson finite difference method is formulated and computationally tested without providing an error analysis. The latter method is attractive because it conserves the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^2$$\end{document}L2 norm in a discrete way, and several preliminary numerical tests on the problem at hand, indicated that a conservative method is more preferable than a non conservative one. Here, we, also, consider a linearization of the Backward Euler method (see [12]), even thought it is dissipative, in order to compare the methods both in theory and practice.

We close the introduction by giving a brief outline of the paper. In Section 2 we define and estimate the consistency error of the time discretization of our numerical methods. In Section 3 we investigate the convergence of the (LBEFE) and (LCNFE) methods by deriving a priori error estimates in the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^2({\varOmega })$$\end{document}L2(Ω) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H^1({\varOmega })$$\end{document}H1(Ω) norms. Finally, we expose results from numerical experiments in Section 4, and give a summary of our results in Section 5.

Consistency Error of the Time-Discretization

Here, we define the consistency error of the time-discretization employed by the (LBEFE) and (LCNFE) methods, which then is estimated in the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^2({\varOmega })$$\end{document}L2(Ω) norm by using Taylor expansions.

Time-Discretization in the (LCNFE) Method

Proposition 2.2

Let us assume that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u\in C^2([0,T],L^2({\varOmega }))\cap C([0,T],{\mathbb {H}}^2({\varOmega }))$$\end{document}u∈C2([0,T],L2(Ω))∩C([0,T],H2(Ω)), and let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\xi _n)_{n=1}^{\scriptscriptstyle N}$$\end{document}(ξn)n=1N be the consistency errors defined by (2.6). Then, it holds that2.7\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Vert \xi _n\Vert \le \,{\mathfrak c}_{\texttt {LCN}}(u)\,\textsf{Q}_{\scriptscriptstyle {\texttt {LCN}}}^n(u), \quad n=1,\dots ,N, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}‖ξn‖≤cLCN(u)QLCNn(u),n=1,⋯,N,where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathfrak c}_{\texttt {LCN}}(u):=\max \big \{1, |\mu |\,\tfrac{5+4\,\beta }{2} \max \limits _{\scriptscriptstyle [0,T]}|u|_{\infty }^{\beta }\big \}$$\end{document}cLCN(u):=max{1,|μ|5+4β2max[0,T]|u|∞β} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textsf{Q}_{\scriptscriptstyle {\texttt {LCN}}}^n(u):=\int _{t_{n-1}}^{t_n} (\Vert u_{t}(s,\cdot )\Vert +\Vert u_{tt}(s,\cdot )\Vert )\,\;ds$$\end{document}QLCNn(u):=∫tn-1n(‖ut(s,·)‖+‖utt(s,·)‖)ds.

Convergence Analysis

In this section, we investigate the convergence in the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^2({\varOmega })$$\end{document}L2(Ω) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H^1({\varOmega })$$\end{document}H1(Ω) norms of the (LBEFE) and (LCNFE) methods. The basic ingredient of the analysis we develop is the construction and the error estimation of a modified version of the aforementioned methods, where the nonlinear term is mollified properly by a smooth cut-off function.

An Auxiliary Smooth Cut-Off Function.

Convergence of the (LBEFE) Method

To handle the nonlinearity of the problem, we introduce below a modified version of the (LBEFE) method.

Theorem 3.1

Let us assume that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u\in C^2([0,T],L^2({\varOmega }))\cap C^1([0,T],{\mathbb {H}}^2({\varOmega }))$$\end{document}u∈C2([0,T],L2(Ω))∩C1([0,T],H2(Ω)), \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f\in C([0,T],L^2({\varOmega }))$$\end{document}f∈C([0,T],L2(Ω)) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$V\in L^{\infty }({\varOmega })$$\end{document}V∈L∞(Ω), and let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varphi _{\star }$$\end{document}φ⋆ be the function defined by (1.14). Then, there exist positive constants \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textsf{C}_{\texttt {LBE}}^{\mathfrak 1}$$\end{document}CLBE1, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textsf{C}_{\texttt Proof

Convergence of the (LCNFE) Method

Proceeding in a similar manner, we consider a modified version of the (LCNFE) method, which is as follows.

Proof

For our convenience, we simplify the notation by setting \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\eta ^m:=u^m-\textsf{R}_h(u^m)$$\end{document}ηm:=um-Rh(um) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta _h^m:=\textsf{R}_h(u^m)-\textsf{W}_{{\lambda _{\star }},h}^m$$\end{document}θhm:=Rh(um)-Wλ⋆,hm for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$m=0,\dots ,N$$\end{document}m=0,⋯,N, where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\lambda _{\star }}:=1+6\,\max \limits _{\scriptscriptstyle [0,T]}|u|_{\infty }$$\end{document}λ⋆:=1+6max[0,T]|u|∞. Numerical Results

We implemented the (LBEFE) and (LCNFE) methods in a Python 3.7.0 program, where the resulting linear systems of algebraic equations are solved by applying the subroutine gmres of the library scipy.sparse.linalg, which is an implementation of the usual Generalized Minimum Residuals (GMRES) method. Also, to compute the elliptic projection of the initial condition \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u_0$$\end{document}u0, we solve the corresponding linear system of algebraic equations by applying the subroutine cg of the latter library, which is based on the Conjugate Gradient (CG) method.

When the exact solution to the problem is given, we compute the numerical approximations errors\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \textsf{E}_{\texttt {DL2}}(N,h):=\max \limits _{1\le {n}\le {\scriptscriptstyle N}} \,\Vert \textsf{U}_h^n-u^n\Vert \quad \text {and}\quad \textsf{E}_{\texttt {DH1}}(N,h):=\max \limits _{1\le {n}\le {\scriptscriptstyle N}} \,\Vert \textsf{U}_h^n-u^n\Vert _{\scriptscriptstyle 1}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}EDL2(N,h):=max1≤n≤N‖Uhn-un‖andEDH1(N,h):=max1≤n≤N‖Uhn-un‖1,where the norm-integrals are approximated by employing a numerical quadrature technique based on a high order Gauss quadrature rule. Choosing \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$h=\textsf{h}(N)$$\end{document}h=h(N), where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textsf{h}(N):=q\,\,N^{-\nu }$$\end{document}h(N):=qN-ν with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$q,\nu >0$$\end{document}q,ν>0, we compute the experimental order of convergence \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textsf{R}(N_1,N_2)$$\end{document}R(N1,N2) with respect to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N^{-1}$$\end{document}N-1, corresponding to given values \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N_1$$\end{document}N1 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N_2$$\end{document}N2 of N, by\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \textsf{R}(N_1,N_2):=\tfrac{ \ln \left[ \textsf{E}\left( N_1,\textsf{h}(N_1)\right) / \textsf{E}\left( N_2,\textsf{h}(N_2)\right) \right] }{\ln (N_2/N_1)}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}R(N1,N2):=lnEN1,h(N1)/EN2,h(N2)ln(N2/N1),where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textsf{E}=\textsf{E}_{\texttt {DL2}}$$\end{document}E=EDL2 or \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textsf{E}_{\texttt {DH1}}$$\end{document}EDH1.

As usual, we determine the initial condition \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u_0$$\end{document}u0 and the load f by considering that the function\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} u(t,x) = \tfrac{1}{2}\exp \left( \tfrac{\textrm{i}}{2} \left( t - \tfrac{1}{2}\right) ^2 \textsf{sgn}\left( t - \tfrac{1}{2}\right) \right) \,(1 - x_1^2)\,(1 - x_2^2)\, \left[ x_1^2\,\textsf{sgn}(x_1)+x_2^2\,\textsf{sgn}(x_2)\right] \end{aligned}$$\end{document}u(t,x)=12expi2t-122sgnt-12(1-x12)(1-x22)x12sgn(x1)+x22sgn(x2)is the exact solution to (1.2)-(1.4). In particular, by choosing \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$h=\tau ^{\frac{1}{2}}$$\end{document}h=τ12 we conclude a first order convergence in the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^2({\varOmega })$$\end{document}L2(Ω) norm with respect to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tau $$\end{document}τ which is in agreement with our error estimates. Also, setting \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$h=\tau $$\end{document}h=τ we get again a first order convergence in the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H^1({\varOmega })$$\end{document}H1(Ω) norm with respect to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tau $$\end{document}τ which is higher than that have shown in our analysis.
